# Staged glossary terms

This is the alphabetical working queue used during the daily audio review.

A term stays here until its canonical wording, AKA terms, short definition, full definition, inline terms, related words, and any obvious taxonomy decisions are ready.

When a term is approved at home, move its complete information to `confirmed-terms.md` and remove its top-level section from this file in the same Git patch.

Categories and learning tracks are optional. Unclear taxonomy must not block promotion.

---

# 10-Percent Doubling Rule

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `10-percent-doubling-rule`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### 10-Percent Doubling Rule

#### Definition

10-Percent Doubling Rule is a race-cube shortcut: the player on roll can generally double when their adjusted count is within about 10 percent of the other player’s count. Knowing this term makes it easier to follow backgammon lessons and analysis.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Current BMS:** `10-Percent Doubling Rule`

## Alias and spelling candidates

### Super 10 Rule

#### Source and treatment notes

- Seen in or proposed from: Comprehensive term list and user editorial direction.
- Proposed treatment: approved alias of 10-Percent Doubling Rule.

## Related words

- Double
- Doubling Cube
- On Roll
- Pip Count
- Race
- 8-9-12 Doubling Rule
- Liby's Rule

## Audio review notes

### Confirmed direction

- Applies to a pure race.
- Ten percent of the leader's pip count estimates the middle of the doubling window.
- It is a simplification of the 8-9-12 Doubling Rule.
- At a leader's pip count of 60, ten percent is 6 pips.

### Still unresolved

- A 5-pip lead was described both as a weak double and as too weak to double.
- The numerical example must be resolved before promotion.

## Decision notes

- Canonical term:
- Aliases to keep or add: Super 10 Rule
- Aliases to reject:
- Definition changes:
- Terms that must remain separate:
- Ready for JSON patch: No
- Delete this section after merge: No

# Acey-Deucey

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `acey-deucey`

**Category:** variants and history

## Word to use

### Acey-Deucey

#### Definition

In backgammon, Acey-Deucey refers to a family of backgammon variants, often associated with U.S. military play, in which 1-2 has special powers. The rules or history are different from standard modern backgammon.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Current BMS:** `Acey-Deucey`, `Binache`, `Catalin`
- **Gammoned Glossary:** `Acey-Deucey`
- **Wiktionary:** `Acey-deucey`

## Alias and spelling candidates

### Binache

#### Source and treatment notes

- Seen in or proposed from: Current BMS
- Current treatment: existing BMS alias of this canonical entry.

### Catalin

#### Source and treatment notes

- Seen in or proposed from: Current BMS
- Current treatment: existing BMS alias of this canonical entry.

## Related words

- Modern Backgammon
- Variant
- Long Backgammon
- Tavla
- Mexican Backgammon
- Takhteh
- Normal Game

## Audio review notes

### Confirmed direction

- Acey-Deucey can describe a roll of 1 and 2.
- The roll moves only 3 pips and is often considered weak in standard backgammon.
- In the Acey-Deucey variant, rolling 1-2 allows the player to choose a double, play it, and then roll again.
- The variant is associated with U.S. military play.

### Still unresolved

- Review the existing Binache and Catalin aliases.
- Decide whether the roll and the family of variants need separate entries.
- Verify the preferred historical wording before promotion.

## Decision notes

- Canonical term:
- Aliases to keep or add:
- Aliases to reject:
- Definition changes:
- Terms that must remain separate:
- Ready for JSON patch: No
- Delete this section after merge: No

# Anchor

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `anchor`

**Category:** strategy and position types

## Word to use

### Anchor

#### Definition

Anchor is a made point in the other player’s home board or outer board that gives safety and contact. The term gives players a shared way to talk about this part of the game.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Backgammon Rules:** `Anchor`
- **Backgammon.com:** `Anchor`
- **Current BMS:** `Anchor`, `Holding Point`
- **Deluxe Backgammon:** `Anchor`
- **Gammoned Glossary:** `Anchor`
- **Wiktionary:** `Anchor`

## Alias and spelling candidates

### Holding Point

#### Source and treatment notes

- Seen in or proposed from: Current BMS
- Current treatment: existing BMS alias of this canonical entry.

## Related words

- Backgammon Board
- Home Board
- Made Point
- Outer Board
- Point
- Forward Anchor
- Front a Prime

## Audio review notes

### Approved short-definition draft

An anchor is a made point in your opponent's board that gives your checkers safety.

### Full-definition points to resolve

- A home-board anchor gives a safe place to re-enter and helps prevent a complete closeout.
- Low anchors were described as the opponent's 24-point and 23-point.
- The butterfly anchor was described as the opponent's 22-point.
- High anchors were described as the opponent's 21-point and 20-point.
- Decide how forward anchors and outer-board anchors fit this classification.
- Decide whether anchor types belong in the full definition or only under related words.

## Decision notes

- Canonical term:
- Aliases to keep or add:
- Aliases to reject:
- Definition changes:
- Terms that must remain separate:
- Ready for JSON patch: No
- Delete this section after merge: No

# Backgammon

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `backgammon`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Backgammon

#### Definition

The term Backgammon refers to the game itself, or a triple-value win when the loser has borne off nothing and still has a checker on the bar or in the winner’s home board. Knowing this term makes it easier to follow backgammon lessons and analysis.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Backgammon Rules:** `Backgammon`
- **Backgammon.com:** `Backgammon`
- **Current BMS:** `Backgammon`, `Triple Game`
- **Deluxe Backgammon:** `Backgammon`
- **Gammoned Glossary:** `Backgammon`
- **Wiktionary:** `Backgammon`, `Triple game`

# Bear Off

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `bear-off`

# Crawford Rule

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `crawford-rule`

**Category:** cube and scoring

## Word to use

### Crawford Rule

#### Definition

Crawford Rule applies in match play. The first game after either player reaches one point from winning the match is played without the doubling cube. Knowing the rule makes it easier to understand cube decisions near the end of a match.

# Deuce-Point Game

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `eject`

# Elimination Format

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `elimination-format`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Elimination Format

#### Definition

# Enter

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `enter`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

# Equity

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `equity`

# Free Drop

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `free-drop`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Free Drop

#### Definition

Free Drop refers to at certain post-Crawford scores, the trailer’s option to pass an early double with little or no match-equity cost. It is useful vocabulary when describing a position, decision, rule, or result.

# Freeze-Out Match

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `freeze-out-match`

**Category:** match and tournament play

## Word to use

### Freeze-Out Match

# Full Prime

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `full-prime`

**Category:** strategy and position types

## Word to use

### Full Prime

#### Definition

Full Prime means six consecutive made points, which no opposing checker can jump in one roll. Players may see this term in strategy discussions, match records, or analysis software.

# Gamesmanship

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `gamesmanship`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Gamesmanship

#### Definition

Gamesmanship refers to conduct intended to gain a competitive edge through pressure or distraction rather than better play. This term belongs in a rules, ethics, or historical explanation and should not be presented as acceptable conduct.

#### Usage note

Include only in an ethics or historical context, not as recommended conduct.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Current BMS:** `Gamesmanship`

# Gammon

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `gammon`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Gammon

#### Definition

Players use Gammon to describe a double-value win in which the loser has not borne off any checker. Players may see this term in strategy discussions, match records, or analysis software.

# Golden Anchor

**Status:** New candidate; perspective-specific anchor term

**Working slug:** `golden-anchor`

**Category:** strategy and position types

## Word to use

### Golden Anchor

#### Definition

A golden anchor is an anchor on your opponent's five-point, which is your twenty-point. It is generally considered the strongest anchor because it provides safety, outfield influence, and strong resistance to an opposing prime.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Backgammon.com:** `Golden Anchor`
- **Deluxe Backgammon:** `Golden Anchor`
- **Comprehensive term list:** `Golden Anchor`

## Alias and spelling candidates

No alias candidate has been identified yet.

## Separation or merge warnings

- Golden Anchor describes holding the opponent's five-point; Golden Point names the five-point itself. Review whether the glossary should keep both perspective-specific terms.

## Related words

- Golden Point
- Five-Point
- Anchor
- High Anchor
- Advanced Anchor
- Holding Game
- Prime
- Outfield Control

# Gul Bara

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `gul-bara`

**Category:** variants and history

## Word to use

### Gul Bara

#### Definition

# Half-Crossover Method

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `half-crossover-method`

**Category:** race and bearoff

## Word to use

### Half-Crossover Method

#### Definition

In backgammon, Half-Crossover Method refers to a quick bearoff estimate that counts approximate half-board movements instead of every pip. Knowing this term makes it easier to follow backgammon lessons and analysis.

# High Anchor

**Status:** New candidate; may overlap Advanced Anchor

**Working slug:** `high-anchor`

**Category:** strategy and position types

## Word to use

### High Anchor

#### Definition

A high anchor is an anchor on your opponent's four-point or five-point, corresponding to your twenty-one-point or twenty-point. High anchors are strong holding-game assets because they provide safety and influence the opponent's outer board.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Backgammon.com:** `High Anchor`
- **Comprehensive term list:** `High Anchor`

## Alias and spelling candidates

### Advanced Anchor

#### Source and treatment notes

- Review as a likely synonym or overlapping teaching category rather than approving an alias automatically.

# Hit and Cover

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `hit-and-cover`

**Category:** checker play and tactics

## Word to use

### Hit and Cover

#### Definition

Hit and Cover means hitting one of the other player’s blots with part of the roll and covering one of your own blots with the rest. The move combines an attack with an immediate improvement in safety.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Current BMS:** `Hit and Cover`
- **Gammoned Glossary:** `Hit and Cover`

# Irish

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `irish`

**Category:** variants and history

## Word to use

### Irish

#### Definition

Irish is an optional variant in which doubles are played only twice rather than four times. The rules or history are different from standard modern backgammon.

# Jacoby Paradox

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `jacoby-paradox`

**Category:** cube and scoring

## Word to use

### Jacoby Paradox

#### Definition

# Jacoby Rule

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `jacoby-rule`

**Category:** cube and scoring

## Word to use

### Jacoby Rule

#### Definition

Jacoby Rule is used in money play. Gammons and backgammons count only after one player has turned the doubling cube. The rule changes the value of playing on for extra points before the cube has been used.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Backgammon Rules:** `Jacoby rule`
- **Backgammon.com:** `Jacoby Rule`
- **Current BMS:** `Jacoby Rule`
- **Gammoned Glossary:** `Jacoby Rule`
- **Wiktionary:** `Jacoby rule`

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `kauder-paradox`

**Status:** New candidate; may overlap Deep Anchor

**Working slug:** `low-anchor`

#### Definition

**Working slug:** `point-on-a-blot`

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `progressive-consolation`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Progressive Consolation

#### Definition

Progressive Consolation is a consolation format in which players enter at different stages according to when they lost in the main event. It is useful vocabulary when describing a position, decision, rule, or result.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Current BMS:** `Progressive Consolation`

# Pure Race

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `pure-race`

**Category:** race and bearoff

## Word to use

### Pure Race

#### Definition

In backgammon, Pure Race refers to a position with no remaining contact, so only checker movement and bearoff efficiency matter. Players may see this term in strategy discussions, match records, or analysis software.

### Weaver

#### Definition

Weaver is wording for deliberately misplaying to tempt an opponent into taking a later double.

# Weaver Coup

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Weaver Coup

#### Definition

In backgammon, Weaver Coup refers to a deceptive too-good cube offer made in hopes that the other player incorrectly takes. Players may see this term in strategy discussions, match records, or analysis software.

# Wisecarver Paradox

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `wisecarver-paradox`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Wisecarver Paradox

#### Definition

In backgammon, Wisecarver Paradox refers to a race situation where the best checker play correctly provokes a double while an inferior play would not. It is useful vocabulary when describing a position, decision, rule, or result.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Current BMS:** `Wisecarver Paradox`

# Woolseys Law for Doubling

**Status:** Existing BMS canonical entry

**Working slug:** `woolseys-law-for-doubling`

**Category:** language, rules, and culture

## Word to use

### Woolseys Law for Doubling

#### Definition

Woolseys Law for Doubling describes if you are unsure whether the other player has a take or pass, that uncertainty is strong evidence that you should double. Knowing this term makes it easier to follow backgammon lessons and analysis.

#### Sources in which this concept or wording appears

- **Backgammon.com:** `Woolsey's Law`
- **Current BMS:** `Woolseys Law for Doubling`
